Well… I guess they’d say I’m here because of the money and the work I was doing on the house. I don’t see it quite the same way, but that’s what they’re saying.

I spent our savings on wood, lights, display pieces, all the things I needed to get it right. It wasn’t just buying junk, you know, it was for the house, for the block even. I kept working on it after dark because I felt like if I stopped, even for the night, we’d miss the chance. Like the whole place had one opportunity and I had to keep it going.

People got upset about it, especially about the savings, but at the time it felt necessary. It felt bigger than just me fixing something up.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I was feeling—well, “energetic” is one way to put it, but it felt better than that. Most days I felt charged up, like there was a current running through me, and honestly almost joyful. Like I was young again, like my body had remembered how it used to be before everything slowed down.

I’d get up and look at the house and just feel this excitement, this push, like, “Come on, let’s get this done, let’s make it right.” It didn’t feel like work in the normal way. It felt important, and I felt alive doing it.

But then, if my wife would come out and say, “You need to stop,” or if one of the contractors questioned how I wanted something done, I’d get hot fast. I mean fast. One minute I’d be feeling great, really up, and then if somebody got in my way or acted like I didn’t know what I was doing, I’d start yelling. I’m not proud of that, but it felt like they were trying to slow down something that needed to happen.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\n(none — this is your first inference)\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess they’d say I’m here because of the money and the work I was doing on the house. I don’t see it quite the same way, but that’s what they’re saying.\n\nI spent our savings on wood, lights, display pieces, all the things I needed to get it right. It wasn’t just buying junk, you know, it was for the house, for the block even. I kept working on it after dark because I felt like if I stopped, even for the night, we’d miss the chance. Like the whole place had one opportunity and I had to keep it going.\n\nPeople got upset about it, especially about the savings, but at the time it felt necessary. It felt bigger than just me fixing something up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you were feeling during the time you were working on the house? Were you feeling unusually energetic, excited, or like you didn’t need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I was feeling—well, “energetic” is one way to put it, but it felt better than that. Most days I felt charged up, like there was a current running through me, and honestly almost joyful. Like I was young again, like my body had remembered how it used to be before everything slowed down.\n\nI’d get up and look at the house and just feel this excitement, this push, like, “Come on, let’s get this done, let’s make it right.” It didn’t feel like work in the normal way. It felt important, and I felt alive doing it.\n\nBut then, if my wife would come out and say, “You need to stop,” or if one of the contractors questioned how I wanted something done, I’d get hot fast. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it wasn’t typical. I mean, I’ve had good days before, sure, everybody has, but this was different. It wasn’t just being in a decent mood or having a little extra pep. It was most days I’d wake up feeling charged up, like somebody had plugged me into the wall overnight. Almost joyful, really. Like I was young again, like my body and my mind were moving faster than they usually do.

At the time, though, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “different” in a bad way. I noticed it, but I thought, well, maybe this is how I’m supposed to feel. Maybe I’d just been slowed down for too long and now I finally had my energy back. It felt right to me while it was happening.

But people around me noticed, especially my wife. She’d say I was too worked up, too intense. And I guess I was, because if she questioned me, or if the contractors pushed back on what I wanted, I’d get hot fast. I’d go from feeling great and excited to yelling before I even had time to think about it. So yes, looking back, it was a change. It was stronger than my normal self.

those signs only started once I felt driven to turn the house into my big civic project.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: The house has gotten hard for my wife and me to live in because my materials and half-finished setups are taking over rooms we need, and people I care about are avoiding me after the arguments.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about three months, since the remodeling started and everything in the house got turned upside down.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 73 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S041\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess they’d say I’m here because of the money and the work I was doing on the house. I don’t see it quite the same way, but that’s what they’re saying.\n\nI spent our savings on wood, lights, display pieces, all the things I needed to get it right. It wasn’t just buying junk, you know, it was for the house, for the block even. I kept working on it after dark because I felt like if I stopped, even for the night, we’d miss the chance. Like the whole place had one opportunity and I had to keep it going.\n\nPeople got upset about it, especially about the savings, but at the time it felt necessary. It felt bigger than just me fixing something up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you were feeling during the time you were working on the house? Were you feeling unusually energetic, excited, or like you didn’t need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I was feeling—well, “energetic” is one way to put it, but it felt better than that. Most days I felt charged up, like there was a current running through me, and honestly almost joyful. Like I was young again, like my body had remembered how it used to be before everything slowed down.\n\nI’d get up and look at the house and just feel this excitement, this push, like, “Come on, let’s get this done, let’s make it right.” It didn’t feel like work in the normal way. It felt important, and I felt alive doing it.\n\nBut then, if my wife would come out and say, “You need to stop,” or if one of the contractors questioned how I wanted something done, I’d get hot fast. I mean fast. One minute I’d be feeling great, really up, and then if somebody got in my way or acted like I didn’t know what I was doing, I’d start yelling. I’m not proud of that, but it felt like they were trying to slow down something that needed to happen.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of being charged up and unusually excited was a change from how you normally feel, or is this something more typical for you? Did you notice it as different at the time?\n\nPatient: No, it wasn’t typical. I mean, I’ve had good days before, sure, everybody has, but this was different. It wasn’t just being in a decent mood or having a little extra pep. It was most days I’d wake up feeling charged up, like somebody had plugged me into the wall overnight. Almost joyful, really. Like I was young again, like my body and my mind were moving faster than they usually do.\n\nAt the time, though, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “different” in a bad way. I noticed it, but I thought, well, maybe this is how I’m supposed to feel. Maybe I’d just been slowed down for too long and now I finally had my energy back. It felt right to me while it was happening.\n\nBut people around me noticed, especially my wife. She’d say I was too worked up, too intense. And I guess I was, because if she questioned me, or if the contractors pushed back on what I wanted, I’d get hot fast. I’d go from feeling great and excited to yelling before I even had time to think about it. So yes, looking back, it was a change. It was stronger than my normal self.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring the time you were feeling this way, did you find that you needed less sleep than usual, or were able to function well with much less sleep?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… sleep was strange then, but I don’t know that I was counting hours or thinking of it like, “I only need this much.” I wasn’t keeping track the way my wife probably was.

I know I’d stay busy late, and my mind was still on the house even when I went inside. I’d lie there thinking about what needed doing, what supplies we needed, what had to be fixed next. But whether I truly needed less sleep, or whether I was just pushing past being tired because I felt so wound up… I don’t know.

At the time I didn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m exhausted, I have to stop.” It was more like there was still too much to do. But I couldn’t tell you exactly how much I slept or whether it was less than usual in a way I understood then. I just know I wasn’t paying much attention to sleep.

Were you feeling unusually energetic, excited, or like you didn’t need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I was feeling—well, “energetic” is one way to put it, but it felt better than that. Most days I felt charged up, like there was a current running through me, and honestly almost joyful. Like I was young again, like my body had remembered how it used to be before everything slowed down.\n\nI’d get up and look at the house and just feel this excitement, this push, like, “Come on, let’s get this done, let’s make it right.” It didn’t feel like work in the normal way. It felt important, and I felt alive doing it.\n\nBut then, if my wife would come out and say, “You need to stop,” or if one of the contractors questioned how I wanted something done, I’d get hot fast. I mean fast. One minute I’d be feeling great, really up, and then if somebody got in my way or acted like I didn’t know what I was doing, I’d start yelling. I’m not proud of that, but it felt like they were trying to slow down something that needed to happen.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of being charged up and unusually excited was a change from how you normally feel, or is this something more typical for you? Did you notice it as different at the time?\n\nPatient: No, it wasn’t typical. I mean, I’ve had good days before, sure, everybody has, but this was different. It wasn’t just being in a decent mood or having a little extra pep. It was most days I’d wake up feeling charged up, like somebody had plugged me into the wall overnight. Almost joyful, really. Like I was young again, like my body and my mind were moving faster than they usually do.\n\nAt the time, though, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “different” in a bad way. I noticed it, but I thought, well, maybe this is how I’m supposed to feel. Maybe I’d just been slowed down for too long and now I finally had my energy back. It felt right to me while it was happening.\n\nBut people around me noticed, especially my wife. She’d say I was too worked up, too intense. And I guess I was, because if she questioned me, or if the contractors pushed back on what I wanted, I’d get hot fast. I’d go from feeling great and excited to yelling before I even had time to think about it. So yes, looking back, it was a change. It was stronger than my normal self.\n\nDoctor: During the time you were feeling this way, did you find that you needed less sleep than usual, or were able to function well with much less sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… sleep was strange then, but I don’t know that I was counting hours or thinking of it like, “I only need this much.” I wasn’t keeping track the way my wife probably was.\n\nI know I’d stay busy late, and my mind was still on the house even when I went inside. I’d lie there thinking about what needed doing, what supplies we needed, what had to be fixed next. But whether I truly needed less sleep, or whether I was just pushing past being tired because I felt so wound up… I don’t know.\n\nAt the time I didn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m exhausted, I have to stop.” It was more like there was still too much to do. But I couldn’t tell you exactly how much I slept or whether it was less than usual in a way I understood then. I don’t know if I can say that exactly, Doctor. I know what you’re asking, but at the time I wasn’t thinking, “I don’t need sleep anymore,” or anything like that. It wasn’t that clear in my head.

I was just… busy. My mind was busy. I’d be thinking about the house, what had to be done next, what I should’ve done different that day, what I needed to get started on in the morning. And if I was tired, I don’t remember paying much attention to it. I wasn’t sitting there measuring it, you know?

My wife might tell you better than me, because she notices those things. For me, it was more like I didn’t want to stop. I don’t know if that means I needed less sleep, or if I was just pushing myself because I felt wound up and focused on what I was doing. At the time it felt like there was too much to do to be laying around.

those signs only started once I felt driven to turn the house into my big civic project.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: The house has gotten hard for my wife and me to live in because my materials and half-finished setups are taking over rooms we need, and people I care about are avoiding me after the arguments.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about three months, since the remodeling started and everything in the house got turned upside down.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 73 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S041\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of being charged up and unusually excited was a change from how you normally feel, or is this something more typical for you? Did you notice it as different at the time?\n\nPatient: No, it wasn’t typical. I mean, I’ve had good days before, sure, everybody has, but this was different. It wasn’t just being in a decent mood or having a little extra pep. It was most days I’d wake up feeling charged up, like somebody had plugged me into the wall overnight. Almost joyful, really. Like I was young again, like my body and my mind were moving faster than they usually do.\n\nAt the time, though, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “different” in a bad way. I noticed it, but I thought, well, maybe this is how I’m supposed to feel. Maybe I’d just been slowed down for too long and now I finally had my energy back. And I guess I was, because if she questioned me, or if the contractors pushed back on what I wanted, I’d get hot fast. I’d go from feeling great and excited to yelling before I even had time to think about it. So yes, looking back, it was a change. It was stronger than my normal self.\n\nDoctor: During the time you were feeling this way, did you find that you needed less sleep than usual, or were able to function well with much less sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… sleep was strange then, but I don’t know that I was counting hours or thinking of it like, “I only need this much.” I wasn’t keeping track the way my wife probably was.\n\nI know I’d stay busy late, and my mind was still on the house even when I went inside. I’d lie there thinking about what needed doing, what supplies we needed, what had to be fixed next. But whether I truly needed less sleep, or whether I was just pushing past being tired because I felt so wound up… I don’t know.\n\nAt the time I didn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m exhausted, I have to stop.” It was more like there was still too much to do. But I couldn’t tell you exactly how much I slept or whether it was less than usual in a way I understood then. I just know I wasn’t paying much attention to sleep.\n\nDoctor: During this time, did you feel that you needed less sleep than usual, or that you could go without much sleep and still feel fine?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that exactly, Doctor. I know what you’re asking, but at the time I wasn’t thinking, “I don’t need sleep anymore,” or anything like that. It wasn’t that clear in my head.\n\nI was just… busy. My mind was busy. I’d be thinking about the house, what had to be done next, what I should’ve done different that day, what I needed to get started on in the morning. And if I was tired, I don’t remember paying much attention to it. I wasn’t sitting there measuring it, you know?\n\nMy wife might tell you better than me, because she notices those things. For me, it was more like I didn’t want to stop. I don’t know if that means I needed less sleep, or if I was just pushing myself because I felt wound up and focused on what I was doing. At the time it felt like there was too much to do to be laying around.\n\n[Last Question]\nLooking back, do you think you were sleeping significantly less than usual during that time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Looking back… I still don’t know if I can say “significantly,” because I wasn’t watching the clock like that. That’s the honest answer. I wish I could tell you, “Yes, it was four hours,” or “No, it was the same as always,” but I don’t really have that in my head.

I do think I was probably sleeping different than usual, just from how much I was up and how late my mind stayed going. But whether it was a lot less, I’m not sure. I wasn’t lying there thinking, “I only slept this many hours.” I was more focused on getting back to what I was doing and thinking through things.

My wife would probably have a clearer answer, because she pays attention to when I come to bed and when I’m up moving around. For me, it all kind of runs together. I know I didn’t feel like sleep was the main thing on my mind then, but I can’t say for certain how much less I was getting.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess they’d say I’m here because of the money and the work I was doing on the house. I don’t see it quite the same way, but that’s what they’re saying.\n\nI spent our savings on wood, lights, display pieces, all the things I needed to get it right. It wasn’t just buying junk, you know, it was for the house, for the block even. I kept working on it after dark because I felt like if I stopped, even for the night, we’d miss the chance. Like the whole place had one opportunity and I had to keep it going.\n\nPeople got upset about it, especially about the savings, but at the time it felt necessary. It felt bigger than just me fixing something up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you were feeling during the time you were working on the house? Were you feeling unusually energetic, excited, or like you didn’t need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I was feeling—well, “energetic” is one way to put it, but it felt better than that. Most days I felt charged up, like there was a current running through me, and honestly almost joyful. Like I was young again, like my body had remembered how it used to be before everything slowed down.\n\nI’d get up and look at the house and just feel this excitement, this push, like, “Come on, let’s get this done, let’s make it right.” It didn’t feel like work in the normal way. It felt important, and I felt alive doing it.\n\nBut then, if my wife would come out and say, “You need to stop,” or if one of the contractors questioned how I wanted something done, I’d get hot fast. I mean fast. One minute I’d be feeling great, really up, and then if somebody got in my way or acted like I didn’t know what I was doing, I’d start yelling. I’m not proud of that, but it felt like they were trying to slow down something that needed to happen.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of being charged up and unusually excited was a change from how you normally feel, or is this something more typical for you? Did you notice it as different at the time?\n\nPatient: No, it wasn’t typical. I mean, I’ve had good days before, sure, everybody has, but this was different. It wasn’t just being in a decent mood or having a little extra pep. It was most days I’d wake up feeling charged up, like somebody had plugged me into the wall overnight. Almost joyful, really. It felt right to me while it was happening.\n\nBut people around me noticed, especially my wife. She’d say I was too worked up, too intense. And I guess I was, because if she questioned me, or if the contractors pushed back on what I wanted, I’d get hot fast. I’d go from feeling great and excited to yelling before I even had time to think about it. So yes, looking back, it was a change. It was stronger than my normal self.\n\nDoctor: During the time you were feeling this way, did you find that you needed less sleep than usual, or were able to function well with much less sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I just know I wasn’t paying much attention to sleep.\n\nDoctor: During this time, did you feel that you needed less sleep than usual, or that you could go without much sleep and still feel fine?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that exactly, Doctor. I know what you’re asking, but at the time I wasn’t thinking, “I don’t need sleep anymore,” or anything like that. It wasn’t that clear in my head.\n\nI was just… busy. My mind was busy. I’d be thinking about the house, what had to be done next, what I should’ve done different that day, what I needed to get started on in the morning. And if I was tired, I don’t remember paying much attention to it. I wasn’t sitting there measuring it, you know?\n\nMy wife might tell you better than me, because she notices those things. For me, it was more like I didn’t want to stop. I don’t know if that means I needed less sleep, or if I was just pushing myself because I felt wound up and focused on what I was doing. At the time it felt like there was too much to do to be laying around.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, do you think you were sleeping significantly less than usual during that time?\n\nPatient: Looking back… I still don’t know if I can say “significantly,” because I wasn’t watching the clock like that. That’s the honest answer. I wish I could tell you, “Yes, it was four hours,” or “No, it was the same as always,” but I don’t really have that in my head.\n\nI do think I was probably sleeping different than usual, just from how much I was up and how late my mind stayed going. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m still not sure I’d put it that way, Doctor. “Needed less sleep” sounds like I knew that about myself at the time, like I was aware I could get by on less and still be fine. I don’t really remember thinking it through like that.

What I remember more is that sleep just wasn’t something I was paying attention to. I wasn’t saying to myself, “I should go to bed now,” or “I’m tired, I better rest.” I was more wrapped up in what I was doing and what still had to be done. So maybe from the outside it looked like I didn’t need as much sleep, but inside my own head it was more like I wasn’t focused on it.

I can’t honestly say, “Yes, I felt like I needed less sleep than usual.” I can say I wasn’t making sleep much of a priority, and I didn’t notice tiredness the way I probably should have. But whether that was really needing less sleep, I don’t know. My wife would probably have a better sense of it than I do.

I know what you’re asking, but at the time I wasn’t thinking, “I don’t need sleep anymore,” or anything like that. It wasn’t that clear in my head.\n\nI was just… busy. My mind was busy. I’d be thinking about the house, what had to be done next, what I should’ve done different that day, what I needed to get started on in the morning. And if I was tired, I don’t remember paying much attention to it. I wasn’t sitting there measuring it, you know?\n\nMy wife might tell you better than me, because she notices those things. For me, it was more like I didn’t want to stop. I don’t know if that means I needed less sleep, or if I was just pushing myself because I felt wound up and focused on what I was doing. At the time it felt like there was too much to do to be laying around.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, do you think you were sleeping significantly less than usual during that time?\n\nPatient: Looking back… I still don’t know if I can say “significantly,” because I wasn’t watching the clock like that. But whether it was a lot less, I’m not sure. I wasn’t lying there thinking, “I only slept this many hours.” I was more focused on getting back to what I was doing and thinking through things.\n\nMy wife would probably have a clearer answer, because she pays attention to when I come to bed and when I’m up moving around. For me, it all kind of runs together. I know I didn’t feel like sleep was the main thing on my mind then, but I can’t say for certain how much less I was getting.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you feel like you needed less sleep than usual during that time, even if you weren’t keeping track of the exact hours?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d put it that way, Doctor. “Needed less sleep” sounds like I knew that about myself at the time, like I was aware I could get by on less and still be fine. I don’t really remember thinking it through like that.\n\nWhat I remember more is that sleep just wasn’t something I was paying attention to. I wasn’t saying to myself, “I should go to bed now,” or “I’m tired, I better rest.” I was more wrapped up in what I was doing and what still had to be done. So maybe from the outside it looked like I didn’t need as much sleep, but inside my own head it was more like I wasn’t focused on it.\n\nI can’t honestly say, “Yes, I felt like I needed less sleep than usual.” I can say I wasn’t making sleep much of a priority, and I didn’t notice tiredness the way I probably should have. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I can say that, no. Not “days without much sleep” and still feeling just as good as if I’d had a full night — that sounds too exact, and I don’t want to tell you something just because it fits the question.

What I can say is, at that time, I wasn’t thinking about sleep the normal way. I’d keep going, and my mind would stay on things, and I might not notice being tired like I usually would. But whether I was actually going for days on very little sleep and feeling fully energetic and alert… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear memory of it like that.

My wife might have seen it better from the outside. She’d know if I was up half the night or acting like I had energy I shouldn’t have had. From inside my own head, it was more like I was caught up in everything and didn’t slow down enough to judge whether I was rested or not. So I can’t honestly say yes to that the way you’re asking it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I think I can say that more clearly than the sleep part. There has been a stretch where most days I felt different from my usual self. Not just in a normal good mood way, like having a nice breakfast or the sun is out or something. It was more like I was charged up inside, like somebody turned the power back on.

I remember feeling almost joyful at times, like I was young again, like I had more fire in me than I’ve had in years. And I don’t mean that in a poetic way — I really felt lighter, quicker, more alive, like things were opening up and I could take things on. It felt noticeable to me, and I think it was noticeable to my wife too, because I wasn’t acting like my usual older self, if that makes sense.

But it wasn’t only happy. That’s the part I don’t like saying as much. If my wife questioned me, or if the contractors questioned me, I’d get hot fast. I mean fast. I’d go from feeling good and sure about things to yelling, because it felt like they were getting in the way or not understanding. I didn’t have much patience for being challenged. I’d feel this rush of anger come up before I had time to slow it down.

So yes, I’d say there was a clear period where my mood was more elevated — charged up, almost joyful — but also more irritable. And it did feel different from my typical self. I’m not usually like that all the time.

I’d go from feeling great and excited to yelling before I even had time to think about it. So yes, looking back, it was a change. It was stronger than my normal self.\n\nDoctor: During the time you were feeling this way, did you find that you needed less sleep than usual, or were able to function well with much less sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… sleep was strange then, but I don’t know that I was counting hours or thinking of it like, “I only need this much.” I wasn’t keeping track the way my wife probably was.\n\nI know I’d stay busy late, and my mind was still on the house even when I went inside. I’d lie there thinking about what needed doing, what supplies we needed, what had to be fixed next. But whether I truly needed less sleep, or whether I was just pushing past being tired because I felt so wound up… I don’t know.\n\nAt the time I didn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m exhausted, I have to stop.” It was more like there was still too much to do. But I couldn’t tell you exactly how much I slept or whether it was less than usual in a way I understood then. I just know I wasn’t paying much attention to sleep.\n\nDoctor: During this time, did you feel that you needed less sleep than usual, or that you could go without much sleep and still feel fine?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that exactly, Doctor. I know what you’re asking, but at the time I wasn’t thinking, “I don’t need sleep anymore,” or anything like that. It wasn’t that clear in my head.\n\nI was just… busy. My mind was busy. I’d be thinking about the house, what had to be done next, what I should’ve done different that day, what I needed to get started on in the morning. And if I was tired, I don’t remember paying much attention to it. I wasn’t sitting there measuring it, you know?\n\nMy wife might tell you better than me, because she notices those things. For me, it was more like I didn’t want to stop. I don’t know if that means I needed less sleep, or if I was just pushing myself because I felt wound up and focused on what I was doing. At the time it felt like there was too much to do to be laying around.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, do you think you were sleeping significantly less than usual during that time?\n\nPatient: Looking back… I still don’t know if I can say “significantly,” because I wasn’t watching the clock like that. That’s the honest answer. I wish I could tell you, “Yes, it was four hours,” or “No, it was the same as always,” but I don’t really have that in my head.\n\nI do think I was probably sleeping different than usual, just from how much I was up and how late my mind stayed going. But whether it was a lot less, I’m not sure. I wasn’t lying there thinking, “I only slept this many hours.” I was more focused on getting back to what I was doing and thinking through things.\n\nMy wife would probably have a clearer answer, because she pays attention to when I come to bed and when I’m up moving around. For me, it all kind of runs together. I know I didn’t feel like sleep was the main thing on my mind then, but I can’t say for certain how much less I was getting.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you feel like you needed less sleep than usual during that time, even if you weren’t keeping track of the exact hours?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d put it that way, Doctor. “Needed less sleep” sounds like I knew that about myself at the time, like I was aware I could get by on less and still be fine. I don’t really remember thinking it through like that.\n\nWhat I remember more is that sleep just wasn’t something I was paying attention to. I wasn’t saying to myself, “I should go to bed now,” or “I’m tired, I better rest.” I was more wrapped up in what I was doing and what still had to be done. So maybe from the outside it looked like I didn’t need as much sleep, but inside my own head it was more like I wasn’t focused on it.\n\nI can’t honestly say, “Yes, I felt like I needed less sleep than usual.” I can say I wasn’t making sleep much of a priority, and I didn’t notice tiredness the way I probably should have. But whether that was really needing less sleep, I don’t know. My wife would probably have a better sense of it than I do.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you feel that you could go for days without much sleep and still feel just as energetic and alert as if you had slept well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that, no. Not “days without much sleep” and still feeling just as good as if I’d had a full night — that sounds too exact, and I don’t want to tell you something just because it fits the question.\n\nWhat I can say is, at that time, I wasn’t thinking about sleep the normal way. I’d keep going, and my mind would stay on things, and I might not notice being tired like I usually would. But whether I was actually going for days on very little sleep and feeling fully energetic and alert… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear memory of it like that.\n\nMy wife might have seen it better from the outside. She’d know if I was up half the night or acting like I had energy I shouldn’t have had. From inside my own head, it was more like I was caught up in everything and didn’t slow down enough to judge whether I was rested or not. So I can’t honestly say yes to that the way you’re asking it.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was a clear period of time when your mood was consistently more elevated, irritable, or high compared to how you usually feel, and if so, did this mood feel noticeably different from your typical self?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I can say that more clearly than the sleep part. There has been a stretch where most days I felt different from my usual self. Not just in a normal good mood way, like having a nice breakfast or the sun is out or something. It was more like I was charged up inside, like somebody turned the power back on.\n\nI remember feeling almost joyful at times, like I was young again, like I had more fire in me than I’ve had in years. And I don’t mean that in a poetic way — I really felt lighter, quicker, more alive, like things were opening up and I could take things on. It felt noticeable to me, and I think it was noticeable to my wife too, because I wasn’t acting like my usual older self, if that makes sense.\n\nBut it wasn’t only happy. That’s the part I don’t like saying as much. If my wife questioned me, or if the contractors questioned me, I’d get hot fast. I mean fast. I’d go from feeling good and sure about things to yelling, because it felt like they were getting in the way or not understanding. I didn’t have much patience for being challenged. I’d feel this rush of anger come up before I had time to slow it down.\n\nSo yes, I’d say there was a clear period where my mood was more elevated — charged up, almost joyful — but also more irritable. And it did feel different from my typical self. I’m not usually like that all the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My wife would probably have a better sense of it than I do.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you feel that you could go for days without much sleep and still feel just as energetic and alert as if you had slept well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that, no. Not “days without much sleep” and still feeling just as good as if I’d had a full night — that sounds too exact, and I don’t want to tell you something just because it fits the question.\n\nWhat I can say is, at that time, I wasn’t thinking about sleep the normal way. I’d keep going, and my mind would stay on things, and I might not notice being tired like I usually would. But whether I was actually going for days on very little sleep and feeling fully energetic and alert… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear memory of it like that.\n\nMy wife might have seen it better from the outside. She’d know if I was up half the night or acting like I had energy I shouldn’t have had. From inside my own head, it was more like I was caught up in everything and didn’t slow down enough to judge whether I was rested or not. So I can’t honestly say yes to that the way you’re asking it.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was a clear period of time when your mood was consistently more elevated, irritable, or high compared to how you usually feel, and if so, did this mood feel noticeably different from your typical self?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I can say that more clearly than the sleep part. There has been a stretch where most days I felt different from my usual self. Not just in a normal good mood way, like having a nice breakfast or the sun is out or something. It was more like I was charged up inside, like somebody turned the power back on.\n\nI remember feeling almost joyful at times, like I was young again, like I had more fire in me than I’ve had in years. And I don’t mean that in a poetic way — I really felt lighter, quicker, more alive, like things were opening up and I could take things on. It felt noticeable to me, and I think it was noticeable to my wife too, because I wasn’t acting like my usual older self, if that makes sense.\n\nBut it wasn’t only happy. That’s the part I don’t like saying as much. If my wife questioned me, or if the contractors questioned me, I’d get hot fast. I mean fast. I’d go from feeling good and sure about things to yelling, because it felt like they were getting in the way or not understanding. I didn’t have much patience for being challenged. I’d feel this rush of anger come up before I had time to slow it down.\n\nSo yes, I’d say there was a clear period where my mood was more elevated — charged up, almost joyful — but also more irritable. And it did feel different from my typical self. I’m not usually like that all the time.\n\n[Last Question]\nWas there a time when this elevated, charged-up mood lasted for at least a full week, without breaking, where most days felt this way to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 73 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days I feel charged up and almost joyful, like I’m young again, but if my wife or the contractors question me, I get hot fast and start yelling.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think it did go on like that for most days, at least for a stretch. I’m trying to be careful with the “full week without breaking” part, because I wasn’t marking it on a calendar or anything, but it wasn’t just one afternoon or one good day.

It felt like day after day I had that charged-up feeling — almost joyful, like I was young again, like I had more energy in me and more certainty about everything. I remember waking up, or just moving through the day, feeling like things were clear and I was ready to take them on. That part felt good, honestly. It felt better than I usually feel.

But then, if my wife questioned me, or if the contractors questioned what I wanted, I’d get hot fast. I mean, I’d go from that high, fired-up feeling to yelling before I even really thought about it. It was like I couldn’t stand being slowed down or second-guessed.

So yes, most days during that period felt that way to me — charged up, almost joyful, and also quick to anger. I can’t swear to the exact number of days without a break, but it was more than just a passing mood.

It wasn’t just buying junk, you know, it was for the house, for the block even. I kept working on it after dark because I felt like if I stopped, even for the night, we’d miss the chance. Like the whole place had one opportunity and I had to keep it going.\n\nPeople got upset about it, especially about the savings, but at the time it felt necessary. It felt bigger than just me fixing something up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you were feeling during the time you were working on the house? Were you feeling unusually energetic, excited, or like you didn’t need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I was feeling—well, “energetic” is one way to put it, but it felt better than that. Most days I felt charged up, like there was a current running through me, and honestly almost joyful. Like I was young again, like my body had remembered how it used to be before everything slowed down.\n\nI’d get up and look at the house and just feel this excitement, this push, like, “Come on, let’s get this done, let’s make it right.” It didn’t feel like work in the normal way. It felt important, and I felt alive doing it.\n\nBut then, if my wife would come out and say, “You need to stop,” or if one of the contractors questioned how I wanted something done, I’d get hot fast. I mean fast. One minute I’d be feeling great, really up, and then if somebody got in my way or acted like I didn’t know what I was doing, I’d start yelling. I’m not proud of that, but it felt like they were trying to slow down something that needed to happen.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of being charged up and unusually excited was a change from how you normally feel, or is this something more typical for you? Did you notice it as different at the time?\n\nPatient: No, it wasn’t typical. I mean, I’ve had good days before, sure, everybody has, but this was different. It wasn’t just being in a decent mood or having a little extra pep. It was most days I’d wake up feeling charged up, like somebody had plugged me into the wall overnight. Almost joyful, really. Like I was young again, like my body and my mind were moving faster than they usually do.\n\nAt the time, though, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “different” in a bad way. I noticed it, but I thought, well, maybe this is how I’m supposed to feel. Maybe I’d just been slowed down for too long and now I finally had my energy back. It felt right to me while it was happening.\n\nBut people around me noticed, especially my wife. She’d say I was too worked up, too intense. And I guess I was, because if she questioned me, or if the contractors pushed back on what I wanted, I’d get hot fast. I’d go from feeling great and excited to yelling before I even had time to think about it. So yes, looking back, it was a change. It was stronger than my normal self.\n\nDoctor: During the time you were feeling this way, did you find that you needed less sleep than usual, or were able to function well with much less sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… sleep was strange then, but I don’t know that I was counting hours or thinking of it like, “I only need this much.” I wasn’t keeping track the way my wife probably was.\n\nI know I’d stay busy late, and my mind was still on the house even when I went inside. I’d lie there thinking about what needed doing, what supplies we needed, what had to be fixed next. But whether I truly needed less sleep, or whether I was just pushing past being tired because I felt so wound up… I don’t know.\n\nAt the time I didn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m exhausted, I have to stop.” It was more like there was still too much to do. But I couldn’t tell you exactly how much I slept or whether it was less than usual in a way I understood then. I just know I wasn’t paying much attention to sleep.\n\nDoctor: During this time, did you feel that you needed less sleep than usual, or that you could go without much sleep and still feel fine?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that exactly, Doctor. I know what you’re asking, but at the time I wasn’t thinking, “I don’t need sleep anymore,” or anything like that. It wasn’t that clear in my head.\n\nI was just… busy. My mind was busy. I’d be thinking about the house, what had to be done next, what I should’ve done different that day, what I needed to get started on in the morning. And if I was tired, I don’t remember paying much attention to it. I wasn’t sitting there measuring it, you know?\n\nMy wife might tell you better than me, because she notices those things. For me, it was more like I didn’t want to stop. I don’t know if that means I needed less sleep, or if I was just pushing myself because I felt wound up and focused on what I was doing. At the time it felt like there was too much to do to be laying around.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, do you think you were sleeping significantly less than usual during that time?\n\nPatient: Looking back… I still don’t know if I can say “significantly,” because I wasn’t watching the clock like that. That’s the honest answer. I wish I could tell you, “Yes, it was four hours,” or “No, it was the same as always,” but I don’t really have that in my head.\n\nI do think I was probably sleeping different than usual, just from how much I was up and how late my mind stayed going. But whether it was a lot less, I’m not sure. I wasn’t lying there thinking, “I only slept this many hours.” I was more focused on getting back to what I was doing and thinking through things.\n\nMy wife would probably have a clearer answer, because she pays attention to when I come to bed and when I’m up moving around. For me, it all kind of runs together. I know I didn’t feel like sleep was the main thing on my mind then, but I can’t say for certain how much less I was getting.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you feel like you needed less sleep than usual during that time, even if you weren’t keeping track of the exact hours?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d put it that way, Doctor. “Needed less sleep” sounds like I knew that about myself at the time, like I was aware I could get by on less and still be fine. I don’t really remember thinking it through like that.\n\nWhat I remember more is that sleep just wasn’t something I was paying attention to. I wasn’t saying to myself, “I should go to bed now,” or “I’m tired, I better rest.” I was more wrapped up in what I was doing and what still had to be done. So maybe from the outside it looked like I didn’t need as much sleep, but inside my own head it was more like I wasn’t focused on it.\n\nI can’t honestly say, “Yes, I felt like I needed less sleep than usual.” I can say I wasn’t making sleep much of a priority, and I didn’t notice tiredness the way I probably should have. I’d keep going, and my mind would stay on things, and I might not notice being tired like I usually would. Yes… I’d say it was clearly different from my usual self. I’m still a little careful saying “for at least a week straight” like I had a stopwatch on it, but it wasn’t just a few minutes here and there. Most days, and for a lot of the day, I felt charged up in a way I don’t normally feel.

It was almost joyful sometimes, honestly. Like I was younger again, like I had this push in me and things felt possible, and I was more certain about what I wanted. That part felt good, so at the time I don’t think I was sitting there thinking, “Something’s wrong.” I was more thinking, “Finally, I’ve got some life in me.”

But then it could turn so fast. If my wife questioned me, or if the contractors didn’t understand what I wanted or pushed back, I’d get hot immediately. I mean, I’d start yelling before I’d really had time to think it through. And that’s not my normal day-to-day self, not like that, not that quick and intense.

So yes, in the way you’re asking — most of the day, nearly every day, for that stretch — it felt noticeably different from how I usually am.

I can’t swear to the exact number of days without a break, but it was more than just a passing mood.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that during this time, your mood was clearly different from your usual self for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least a week straight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it was clearly different from my usual self. I’m still a little careful saying “for at least a week straight” like I had a stopwatch on it, but it wasn’t just a few minutes here and there. Most days, and for a lot of the day, I felt charged up in a way I don’t normally feel.\n\nIt was almost joyful sometimes, honestly. Like I was younger again, like I had this push in me and things felt possible, and I was more certain about what I wanted. That part felt good, so at the time I don’t think I was sitting there thinking, “Something’s wrong.” I was more thinking, “Finally, I’ve got some life in me.”\n\nBut then it could turn so fast. If my wife questioned me, or if the contractors didn’t understand what I wanted or pushed back, I’d get hot immediately. I mean, I’d start yelling before I’d really had time to think it through. And that’s not my normal day-to-day self, not like that, not that quick and intense.\n\nSo yes, in the way you’re asking — most of the day, nearly every day, for that stretch — it felt noticeably different from how I usually am.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring this time when you were feeling more charged up and intense, did you find yourself pushing harder to get things done, like working on the house more intensely or taking on more projects than usual?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 73 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Increased_Activity\nmanifestation: \"I can’t just sit in the chair anymore; I’m up moving from the garden to the darkroom to the porch, carrying tools and fixing things before anyone asks.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I was pushing a lot harder. I mean, I couldn’t just sit in my chair the way I usually might. Normally I can sit a while, watch something, drink coffee, look out the window — but during that time it was like sitting still felt wrong.

I’d be up and moving around the house, going from one thing to another. I’d start in the garden, then remember something in the darkroom, then end up out on the porch with tools in my hands, fixing something before anybody even asked me to. It wasn’t like I had one neat project and finished it calmly. It was more like I’d see something and think, “That needs doing,” and then I was already doing it.

I was carrying tools around, checking things, tightening things, fussing with the porch, looking at the garden, moving back and forth. My wife would say something like, “Can’t that wait?” and to me it felt like, no, it really couldn’t wait. I had this push in me, like I had to keep going. So yes, I was much more active than usual, much more driven to get things done.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.3, F31.4, F31.5, F31.6, F31.7, F31.8, F31.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess they’d say I’m here because of the money and the work I was doing on the house. I don’t see it quite the same way, but that’s what they’re saying.\n\nI spent our savings on wood, lights, display pieces, all the things I needed to get it right. It wasn’t just buying junk, you know, it was for the house, for the block even. I kept working on it after dark because I felt like if I stopped, even for the night, we’d miss the chance. Like the whole place had one opportunity and I had to keep it going.\n\nPeople got upset about it, especially about the savings, but at the time it felt necessary. It felt bigger than just me fixing something up.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how you were feeling during the time you were working on the house? Were you feeling unusually energetic, excited, or like you didn’t need much sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I was feeling—well, “energetic” is one way to put it, but it felt better than that. Most days I felt charged up, like there was a current running through me, and honestly almost joyful. Like I was young again, like my body had remembered how it used to be before everything slowed down.\n\nI’d get up and look at the house and just feel this excitement, this push, like, “Come on, let’s get this done, let’s make it right.” It didn’t feel like work in the normal way. It felt important, and I felt alive doing it.\n\nBut then, if my wife would come out and say, “You need to stop,” or if one of the contractors questioned how I wanted something done, I’d get hot fast. I mean fast. One minute I’d be feeling great, really up, and then if somebody got in my way or acted like I didn’t know what I was doing, I’d start yelling. I’m not proud of that, but it felt like they were trying to slow down something that needed to happen.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of being charged up and unusually excited was a change from how you normally feel, or is this something more typical for you? Did you notice it as different at the time?\n\nPatient: No, it wasn’t typical. I mean, I’ve had good days before, sure, everybody has, but this was different. It wasn’t just being in a decent mood or having a little extra pep. It was most days I’d wake up feeling charged up, like somebody had plugged me into the wall overnight. Almost joyful, really. Like I was young again, like my body and my mind were moving faster than they usually do.\n\nAt the time, though, I don’t know if I would’ve called it “different” in a bad way. I noticed it, but I thought, well, maybe this is how I’m supposed to feel. Maybe I’d just been slowed down for too long and now I finally had my energy back. It felt right to me while it was happening.\n\nBut people around me noticed, especially my wife. She’d say I was too worked up, too intense. And I guess I was, because if she questioned me, or if the contractors pushed back on what I wanted, I’d get hot fast. I’d go from feeling great and excited to yelling before I even had time to think about it. So yes, looking back, it was a change. It was stronger than my normal self.\n\nDoctor: During the time you were feeling this way, did you find that you needed less sleep than usual, or were able to function well with much less sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean… sleep was strange then, but I don’t know that I was counting hours or thinking of it like, “I only need this much.” I wasn’t keeping track the way my wife probably was.\n\nI know I’d stay busy late, and my mind was still on the house even when I went inside. I’d lie there thinking about what needed doing, what supplies we needed, what had to be fixed next. But whether I truly needed less sleep, or whether I was just pushing past being tired because I felt so wound up… I don’t know.\n\nAt the time I didn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m exhausted, I have to stop.” It was more like there was still too much to do. But I couldn’t tell you exactly how much I slept or whether it was less than usual in a way I understood then. I just know I wasn’t paying much attention to sleep.\n\nDoctor: During this time, did you feel that you needed less sleep than usual, or that you could go without much sleep and still feel fine?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that exactly, Doctor. I know what you’re asking, but at the time I wasn’t thinking, “I don’t need sleep anymore,” or anything like that. It wasn’t that clear in my head.\n\nI was just… busy. My mind was busy. I’d be thinking about the house, what had to be done next, what I should’ve done different that day, what I needed to get started on in the morning. And if I was tired, I don’t remember paying much attention to it. I wasn’t sitting there measuring it, you know?\n\nMy wife might tell you better than me, because she notices those things. For me, it was more like I didn’t want to stop. I don’t know if that means I needed less sleep, or if I was just pushing myself because I felt wound up and focused on what I was doing. At the time it felt like there was too much to do to be laying around.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, do you think you were sleeping significantly less than usual during that time?\n\nPatient: Looking back… I still don’t know if I can say “significantly,” because I wasn’t watching the clock like that. That’s the honest answer. I wish I could tell you, “Yes, it was four hours,” or “No, it was the same as always,” but I don’t really have that in my head.\n\nI do think I was probably sleeping different than usual, just from how much I was up and how late my mind stayed going. But whether it was a lot less, I’m not sure. I wasn’t lying there thinking, “I only slept this many hours.” I was more focused on getting back to what I was doing and thinking through things.\n\nMy wife would probably have a clearer answer, because she pays attention to when I come to bed and when I’m up moving around. For me, it all kind of runs together. I know I didn’t feel like sleep was the main thing on my mind then, but I can’t say for certain how much less I was getting.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you feel like you needed less sleep than usual during that time, even if you weren’t keeping track of the exact hours?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d put it that way, Doctor. “Needed less sleep” sounds like I knew that about myself at the time, like I was aware I could get by on less and still be fine. I don’t really remember thinking it through like that.\n\nWhat I remember more is that sleep just wasn’t something I was paying attention to. I wasn’t saying to myself, “I should go to bed now,” or “I’m tired, I better rest.” I was more wrapped up in what I was doing and what still had to be done. So maybe from the outside it looked like I didn’t need as much sleep, but inside my own head it was more like I wasn’t focused on it.\n\nI can’t honestly say, “Yes, I felt like I needed less sleep than usual.” I can say I wasn’t making sleep much of a priority, and I didn’t notice tiredness the way I probably should have. But whether that was really needing less sleep, I don’t know. My wife would probably have a better sense of it than I do.\n\nDoctor: During that time, did you feel that you could go for days without much sleep and still feel just as energetic and alert as if you had slept well?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that, no. Not “days without much sleep” and still feeling just as good as if I’d had a full night — that sounds too exact, and I don’t want to tell you something just because it fits the question.\n\nWhat I can say is, at that time, I wasn’t thinking about sleep the normal way. I’d keep going, and my mind would stay on things, and I might not notice being tired like I usually would. But whether I was actually going for days on very little sleep and feeling fully energetic and alert… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear memory of it like that.\n\nMy wife might have seen it better from the outside. She’d know if I was up half the night or acting like I had energy I shouldn’t have had. From inside my own head, it was more like I was caught up in everything and didn’t slow down enough to judge whether I was rested or not. So I can’t honestly say yes to that the way you’re asking it.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if there was a clear period of time when your mood was consistently more elevated, irritable, or high compared to how you usually feel, and if so, did this mood feel noticeably different from your typical self?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I can say that more clearly than the sleep part. There has been a stretch where most days I felt different from my usual self. Not just in a normal good mood way, like having a nice breakfast or the sun is out or something. It was more like I was charged up inside, like somebody turned the power back on.\n\nI remember feeling almost joyful at times, like I was young again, like I had more fire in me than I’ve had in years. And I don’t mean that in a poetic way — I really felt lighter, quicker, more alive, like things were opening up and I could take things on. It felt noticeable to me, and I think it was noticeable to my wife too, because I wasn’t acting like my usual older self, if that makes sense.\n\nBut it wasn’t only happy. That’s the part I don’t like saying as much. If my wife questioned me, or if the contractors questioned me, I’d get hot fast. I mean fast. I’d go from feeling good and sure about things to yelling, because it felt like they were getting in the way or not understanding. I didn’t have much patience for being challenged. I’d feel this rush of anger come up before I had time to slow it down.\n\nSo yes, I’d say there was a clear period where my mood was more elevated — charged up, almost joyful — but also more irritable. And it did feel different from my typical self. I’m not usually like that all the time.\n\nDoctor: Was there a time when this elevated, charged-up mood lasted for at least a full week, without breaking, where most days felt this way to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did go on like that for most days, at least for a stretch. I’m trying to be careful with the “full week without breaking” part, because I wasn’t marking it on a calendar or anything, but it wasn’t just one afternoon or one good day.\n\nIt felt like day after day I had that charged-up feeling — almost joyful, like I was young again, like I had more energy in me and more certainty about everything. I remember waking up, or just moving through the day, feeling like things were clear and I was ready to take them on. That part felt good, honestly. It felt better than I usually feel.\n\nBut then, if my wife questioned me, or if the contractors questioned what I wanted, I’d get hot fast. I mean, I’d go from that high, fired-up feeling to yelling before I even really thought about it. It was like I couldn’t stand being slowed down or second-guessed.\n\nSo yes, most days during that period felt that way to me — charged up, almost joyful, and also quick to anger. I can’t swear to the exact number of days without a break, but it was more than just a passing mood.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that during this time, your mood was clearly different from your usual self for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least a week straight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it was clearly different from my usual self. I’m still a little careful saying “for at least a week straight” like I had a stopwatch on it, but it wasn’t just a few minutes here and there. Most days, and for a lot of the day, I felt charged up in a way I don’t normally feel.\n\nIt was almost joyful sometimes, honestly. Like I was younger again, like I had this push in me and things felt possible, and I was more certain about what I wanted. That part felt good, so at the time I don’t think I was sitting there thinking, “Something’s wrong.” I was more thinking, “Finally, I’ve got some life in me.”\n\nBut then it could turn so fast. If my wife questioned me, or if the contractors didn’t understand what I wanted or pushed back, I’d get hot immediately. I mean, I’d start yelling before I’d really had time to think it through. And that’s not my normal day-to-day self, not like that, not that quick and intense.\n\nSo yes, in the way you’re asking — most of the day, nearly every day, for that stretch — it felt noticeably different from how I usually am.\n\nDoctor: During this time when you were feeling more charged up and intense, did you find yourself pushing harder to get things done, like working on the house more intensely or taking on more projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was pushing a lot harder. I mean, I couldn’t just sit in my chair the way I usually might. Normally I can sit a while, watch something, drink coffee, look out the window — but during that time it was like sitting still felt wrong.\n\nI’d be up and moving around the house, going from one thing to another. That’s the part I don’t like saying as much. If my wife questioned me, or if the contractors questioned me, I’d get hot fast. I mean fast. I’d go from feeling good and sure about things to yelling, because it felt like they were getting in the way or not understanding. I didn’t have much patience for being challenged. I’d feel this rush of anger come up before I had time to slow it down.\n\nSo yes, I’d say there was a clear period where my mood was more elevated — charged up, almost joyful — but also more irritable. And it did feel different from my typical self. I’m not usually like that all the time.\n\nDoctor: Was there a time when this elevated, charged-up mood lasted for at least a full week, without breaking, where most days felt this way to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did go on like that for most days, at least for a stretch. I’m trying to be careful with the “full week without breaking” part, because I wasn’t marking it on a calendar or anything, but it wasn’t just one afternoon or one good day.\n\nIt felt like day after day I had that charged-up feeling — almost joyful, like I was young again, like I had more energy in me and more certainty about everything. I remember waking up, or just moving through the day, feeling like things were clear and I was ready to take them on. That part felt good, honestly. It felt better than I usually feel.\n\nBut then, if my wife questioned me, or if the contractors questioned what I wanted, I’d get hot fast. I mean, I’d go from that high, fired-up feeling to yelling before I even really thought about it. It was like I couldn’t stand being slowed down or second-guessed.\n\nSo yes, most days during that period felt that way to me — charged up, almost joyful, and also quick to anger. I can’t swear to the exact number of days without a break, but it was more than just a passing mood.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that during this time, your mood was clearly different from your usual self for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least a week straight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say it was clearly different from my usual self. I’m still a little careful saying “for at least a week straight” like I had a stopwatch on it, but it wasn’t just a few minutes here and there. Most days, and for a lot of the day, I felt charged up in a way I don’t normally feel.\n\nIt was almost joyful sometimes, honestly. Like I was younger again, like I had this push in me and things felt possible, and I was more certain about what I wanted. That part felt good, so at the time I don’t think I was sitting there thinking, “Something’s wrong.” I was more thinking, “Finally, I’ve got some life in me.”\n\nBut then it could turn so fast. If my wife questioned me, or if the contractors didn’t understand what I wanted or pushed back, I’d get hot immediately. I mean, I’d start yelling before I’d really had time to think it through. And that’s not my normal day-to-day self, not like that, not that quick and intense.\n\nSo yes, in the way you’re asking — most of the day, nearly every day, for that stretch — it felt noticeably different from how I usually am.\n\nDoctor: During this time when you were feeling more charged up and intense, did you find yourself pushing harder to get things done, like working on the house more intensely or taking on more projects than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was pushing a lot harder. I’m not sure I can say that clearly. Sleep is a little harder for me to pin down looking back, because I wasn’t really keeping track of the hours, and at my age sometimes sleep is odd anyway.
